- OQ-92 (§6 stage 3 "rationale=null with flag") `matches` has no boolean column for the flag and adding one for a transient state is not worth a schema change, so the outcome lives in `matches.breakdown["rationale_status"]`: `ok` (the model answered), `cached` (reused from the same (opportunity_version, profile_version) row), `below_threshold` (score < 50, never called), `failed` (the model never produced valid JSON within LLM_OUTPUT_RETRIES; `breakdown["rationale_error"]` carries a 300-char reason) and `unavailable` (no ANTHROPIC_API_KEY). `rationale` itself stays NULL for everything but ok/cached, so the frontend can render on `rationale` alone and use the status only to explain WHY it is missing. The failed attempt is still metered: AgentRunner writes the agent_steps row and the usage_ledger llm_* rows under the match's tenant even when the step failed.
- OQ-93 (§6 what re-scores a profile) `profile.changed` is published by PUT /profiles/{id} when a value actually changed AND by every profile sub-resource write, because `bump_version` already fires on all of them and `version` is half the match cache key: leaving a child write unpublished would keep a stale match row forever. The cost is that an onboarding wizard queues one re-score per edit; the job is idempotent and Celery-expiring (3600 s), so the fix if it hurts is debouncing in the worker (coalesce by profile id over a minute), not narrowing which writes count.
- OQ-94 (§6 stage 1 / §10.2 matches) A pair dropped by stage 1 is NOT written. `matches` supports band `filtered` with a `filtered_reason` (M4-01), and a single-notice score keeps that option open for a future "why did I not see this?" view, but the batch path would otherwise store 200 profiles x 50k notices = 10 M rows whose whole content is "no". Only pairs that survive stage 1 get a row; an `ineligible_set_aside` pair IS stored (it is kept with cap 30, not filtered).
- OQ-95 (§12 load target) Measured on the dev compose Postgres, one process scores ~1,800 pairs/second end to end (stage 1 + stage 2 + the JSONB write): 2,000 notices x 20 profiles = 40,000 pairs in ~22 s. SPEC 12's 50k x 200 is 10 M pairs, which a single process would need ~90 minutes for, so the < 10 min target is only reachable by sharding: profiles are embarrassingly parallel, `score_batch(tenant_id=...)` and `bidradar.rescore_profile` already slice that way, and M7-10's load script must fan the batch out per profile (or per tenant) across worker processes. A real corpus also prunes far more in `candidate_opportunities` (region, country, notice type, open, deadline ahead) than this test does, where every pair survives on purpose.
- OQ-96 (§6 learning loop, what a "term" is) SPEC 6 says the weekly job "suggests new include/exclude keywords" without saying where candidate terms come from. `core.matching.learning.candidate_terms` mines unigrams and ADJACENT bigrams from the notice title + summary_ai, lower-cased, minus a small stop list (English function words plus procurement boilerplate: contractor, solicitation, services, shall, ...), tokens under 4 characters and anything starting with a digit; a bigram is never bridged across a dropped word or across two texts. The profile's existing include keywords are added verbatim so a phrase the tokeniser would never produce still gets re-weighed. A term is only proposed with >= 3 observations, at least one observation WITHOUT it (a term in every notice has the baseline as its rate and explains nothing) and |lift| >= 0.15; delta_weight = lift x 2 rounded to one decimal and clamped to +/-2.0, matching the one-decimal scale of profile_keywords.weight. Approving an include suggestion for a term already on the profile NUDGES its weight by delta_weight (clamped to 0.1..9.9) instead of creating a duplicate.
- OQ-97 (§10.3 route naming) The decision route is `PUT /api/v1/profiles/{profile_id}/keyword-suggestions/{id}` - `{id}`, not `{suggestion_id}` - because the audit middleware's default action is "<method> <route template>" in `audit_log.action varchar(64)`, and `{suggestion_id}` makes that string 69 characters, so the audit row silently fails to write and the "every mutating route is audited" test goes red. SPEC 10.3 writes the route the same way. If more long routes appear, widen `audit_log.action` rather than shortening every path. A `GET` companion route is added beyond SPEC 10.3's list so the frontend can show the pending proposals.
- OQ-98 (§6/§7 two channel vocabularies) `core.preferences.NotificationChannel` (email, slack, teams, whatsapp, web_push - what user_notification_prefs.channels_by_event and the settings UI speak) and the dispatcher's channel keys (`notify.registry.CHANNEL_NAMES` = in_app, email, slack, teams, push) are not the same list. Rather than migrate either, `notify.registry.normalize_channels` is the ONE place that maps between them: web_push/webpush -> push, in-app/inapp/bell -> in_app, and `whatsapp` -> dropped, because SPEC 7 lists WhatsApp for India but no channel implementation exists yet and silently delivering it somewhere else would be worse than not delivering it. alert_rules.channels stores the dispatcher spelling; the API accepts either and normalises on the way in (a body whose channels are ALL unusable is 422 rather than a silent no-op).
- OQ-99 (§6 saved searches, how `q` is applied to one notice) A saved search stores the `GET /opportunities` query string verbatim as jsonb, but evaluating an alert rule has ONE notice in hand, so re-running `websearch_to_tsquery` per rule would be a query per rule per match. `core.matching.saved_search.text_matches` therefore applies `q` in Python: punctuation is flattened, terms are matched whole-word, "quoted phrases" stay together and a leading `-` excludes. The consequence is that alert evaluation does NOT stem (a saved search for "services" will not fire on "service") while the search page does; if that divergence bites, the fix is to evaluate rules with one `to_tsvector(...) @@ websearch_to_tsquery(...)` query per match over the rule set rather than to grow the Python matcher. Filters also gain `due_within_days` and `buyer`, which the search route does not have yet - a rolling deadline window is what an alert actually wants, and both are ignored harmlessly by a route that does not know them.
- OQ-100 (§7 / M7-01 OPS_SLACK_WEBHOOK_URL is a credential) A Slack incoming-webhook URL is a bearer credential: anyone holding it can post to the ops channel. It is NOT in `SECRET_SETTINGS`, because that list drives `infra/cloudrun/**` through `python -m app.jobs.generate_cloudrun` and `tests/unit/test_cloudrun_manifests.py` fails on drift, and this worktree must not touch infra/. The heuristic in `test_secret_settings.py` does not flag the name either (no `secret`/`token`/`_key` marker). RECOMMENDED AT MERGE: add `ops_slack_webhook_url` to SECRET_SETTINGS and re-run the generator, exactly as the M4 merge did for sendgrid_api_key / smtp_password / vapid_private_key. `OPS_EMAIL` is an address, not a credential.
- OQ-101 (§7 ops row has no tenant) Every other SPEC 7 row lands in `notifications`, which is tenant- and user-scoped. `adapter.failing` belongs to the PLATFORM: there is no tenant that owns a broken SAM adapter and no user to key idempotency on. The router therefore posts it straight to `OPS_SLACK_WEBHOOK_URL` (Block Kit header + message + run/health context) and sends one email to `OPS_EMAIL` through the configured EmailProvider, writing no notification and no delivery row. Two consequences: the ops path has no retry/fallback ladder (a refusing webhook is logged in the run summary, not retried - the next failing run pages again), and it is invisible in the delivery log. If ops alerts need the same guarantees as tenant ones, the fix is an internal tenant with an ops user (the `internal` tenant from OQ-47 already exists) rather than special-casing the Dispatcher.
- OQ-102 (§7 "medium match -> digest") A medium match creates its notification row immediately (so the in-app bell shows it at once - `InAppChannel` treats the notifications row itself as the bell item) and its EMAIL delivery is queued with `scheduled_for` = the user's next digest instant. That is exactly the shape `notify.digest.collect_digest` already retires ("every still-queued email delivery whose scheduled_for has passed"), so the digest folds it in and `flush_due` never sends it separately. An alert rule in `instant` mode overrides that and the email goes out at once.
- OQ-103 (§10.3 `match` on the opportunity routes) SPEC 10.3 asks GET /opportunities/{id} for "match for the active profile" and OQ-49 left it null "until the matches table lands (M4)". No M4 task owns that read path, so it is folded into M4-15 (the end-to-end test needs it to prove the chain is visible to the UI). "Active profile" is not modelled - a tenant may have several - so the block is the tenant's BEST match for the notice (highest score, then the newest opportunity and profile version), which is what "should we look at this?" actually asks. `min_score` on the list route is now applied as an EXISTS over the caller's own matches, so a notice nobody in the tenant scored is filtered out rather than silently passed; the one placeholder assertion that encoded the old no-op behaviour was replaced with tests that a tenant sees its own match and another tenant sees null for the same global notice.
- OQ-104 (§12 what "Mailpit asserting the email" means in the e2e test) The end-to-end test uses the in-memory EmailProvider and asserts the rendered subject, HTML, text and List-Unsubscribe headers, rather than the compose Mailpit. M4-10 already sends a real message through Mailpit's SMTP and reads it back through its API (marked `mailpit`, skipped when unreachable), so the SMTP hop is covered there; repeating it here would make the end-to-end test skip on any machine without the compose stack, which is exactly the test that must never be skipped. Slack is a respx-intercepted POST to the tenant's real `integrations` row resolved through its `env:` secret_ref, and Teams is left unconfigured on purpose so the run also proves an unconfigured channel is `skipped` rather than failed.

- OQ-110 (§9 / M6-01 who may do what on the board) SPEC 3 gives bid managers "move pursuits"; the finer split is a decision. Pursue and Pass are bid-manager (they start paid agent work / close a card), Watch is writer-and-up (it costs nothing), PATCH is writer-and-up for a FORWARD move but bid-manager for an assignment, a BACKWARDS drag and reopening a terminal pursuit (`app.core.pursuit_stages`: a writer must not undo Gate 1 by dragging a card). Every tenant role reads GET /pursuits.
- OQ-111 (§9 / M6-01 which profile a board action uses) `POST /opportunities/{id}/pursue|watch|pass` take an OPTIONAL `profile_id`. With exactly one active profile the server picks it; with several it answers 409 {error: profile_required, profile_ids[]} rather than guessing, and the UI asks. A tenant with no active profile gets 404.
- OQ-112 (§9 / M6-01 pursue never runs the pipeline inline) Pursue creates the `pipeline` agent run and hands it to Celery; it never falls back to running in the API process the way POST /pursuits/{id}/agents/run does, because a board click must not block on a 30-minute pipeline. Under CELERY_TASK_ALWAYS_EAGER (tests) and when no broker answers, the run row is left `queued` and the response says so in `agents.reason`. A plan whose `agent_budget_usd_month` is 0 (free) opens the card and enqueues nothing.
- OQ-113 (§9 / M6-01 stage vocabulary storage) `pursuits.stage` stays `varchar(32)` with a CHECK listing the eleven SPEC 9 stages (constraint `ck_pursuits_stage`) rather than becoming a Postgres enum: a new stage is then one migration instead of an enum rewrite, and `app.core.pursuit_stages.STAGES` stays the single source of truth for the model, the migration and the API validator. `POST /pursuits/{id}/decision` (Gate 1) is deliberately NOT defined here — it belongs to the parallel bid/no-bid branch; M6 only reads `pursuits.decision`.
- OQ-114 (§9 / M6-02 the Indian EMD offset) SPEC 9's acceptance line reads "IN pursuits add emd_bg_ready and dsc_check (due-3d)", which can be read as both at due-3d. Implemented as emd_bg_ready = due-5d and dsc_check = due-3d: a bank guarantee takes days to raise, a DSC check is a same-week task, and pairing EMD with the internal draft date is what an Indian bid desk actually plans against. One constant in `core.key_dates.DEADLINE_OFFSET_HOURS` if the owner wants both at 3 days.
- OQ-115 (§9 / M6-02 what `now` does in auto_dates) `auto_dates(notice, region, now)` never uses `now` to decide WHICH dates exist — a milestone whose moment has already passed is still created, flagged `is_past`, so the reminder ladder can report it overdue and an amendment that pushes the deadline out makes the same row future again. A notice with no `response_due_at` (forecast, sources sought) gets no derived dates at all.
- OQ-116 (§9 / M6-02 what an amendment does to an acknowledgement) When the buyer moves the deadline, every `source = 'auto'` row shifts, `pursuits.internal_due_at` follows, and any acknowledgement on a SHIFTED row is cleared with the note "the buyer moved the deadline; re-acknowledge this date" — "I have seen this" was about the old date. A row a human created or edited becomes `source = 'user'` and is never moved or cleared again, and an auto kind the notice no longer states (a withdrawn Q&A date) is deleted unless it had been edited.
- OQ-117 (§10.2 / M6-07 table names to reconcile at merge) SPEC 10.2 names the tables `tasks` and `comments`. This branch creates them as `pursuit_tasks` and `pursuit_comments` (models in app/models/collab.py, migration 0011) because the parallel M5 branch is adding its own `tasks` table for the drafters in migration 0007. The orchestrator must pick ONE pair of names at merge; everything here goes through `app.services.collab`, so the rename is that module, `app/models/collab.py`, the migration and the route bodies. The drafters should call the merged `create_task_from_placeholder` rather than writing rows themselves.
- OQ-118 (§3 / M6-07 who may close a task and edit a comment) Creating, reassigning, re-dating and deleting a task is writer-and-up. Closing (and re-opening) a task is open to EVERY tenant role, but only for a task assigned to that user and only when the request changes nothing but `status` — so a reviewer can tick off their own review without being able to rename or reassign work. Commenting and resolving follow SPEC 3's reviewer row (owner / bid manager / writer / reviewer); a viewer reads. Editing or deleting a comment is the author or a bid manager.
- OQ-119 (§8 / M6-07 [NEEDS INPUT] identity) A task made from a placeholder is keyed by (pursuit, agent, case-folded placeholder) in `pursuit_tasks.ref`, so re-running an agent never asks the same question twice and a task somebody already completed stays completed. Two DIFFERENT agents asking for the same fact each get their own task, because each needs it in its own artefact. `[NEEDS INPUT]` with no label becomes "unspecified input", and a placeholder is never allowed to span a line so a stray bracket cannot swallow a paragraph.
- OQ-120 (§10.3 / M6-04 how the iCal link authenticates) A calendar client cannot hold a session, so GET /api/v1/calendar.ics is a PUBLIC route whose `token` query parameter is the credential: an HS256 JWT (AUTH_SECRET, no expiry — a feed URL that dies in two weeks is worse than useless) naming the tenant, the user and a `nonce` that must still equal `user_notification_prefs.calendar_token`. POST /api/v1/me/calendar-token issues or rotates the nonce, which revokes every link handed out before. Nothing is read from the token before the signature verifies, and a bad / foreign / rotated one is 401.
- OQ-121 (§9 / M6-04 whose dates are in the feed) The feed carries the key dates of pursuits the reader OWNS plus those they have a task on (`pursuit_tasks.assignee_user_id`), which is the closest thing the data model has to SPEC 9's "assignees". Times are absolute UTC DTSTART/DTEND with a 30-minute block; the dual time-zone string and the deep link live in the DESCRIPTION, so a client showing its own zone still displays the buyer's clock.
- OQ-122 (§7 / M6-04 where a calendar credential lives) A Google/Outlook connection is PER USER, so it is a `calendar_connections` row (tenant_id + user_id + provider) rather than an `integrations` row (per tenant, M4-11); the OAuth token is a `secret_ref` resolved through app.services.secrets, never a column. There is no OAuth CONSENT flow yet: rows are created out of band (or by the settings UI once the flow exists) and the API only lists and disconnects them. `pursuit_dates.sequence` is an explicit counter bumped on every change, because RFC 5545 requires a strictly higher SEQUENCE for a calendar to accept an updated event and a timestamp-derived number is too coarse for two edits in the same minute.
- OQ-123 (§9 / M6-04 a push failure never blocks the date) Provider pushes are best effort: a `CalendarPushError` is recorded on `calendar_connections.last_error` and `calendar_events.last_error`, logged, and swallowed. The iCal feed is always correct even when a push is not, and a recalculation triggered by an amendment can never be aborted by a calendar outage. A provider event whose key date was deleted while the provider was down is therefore orphaned; a reconciliation sweep is a follow-up.
- OQ-124 (§7 / M6-05 the three WhatsApp gates) WhatsApp Business forbids business-initiated free text, so `WhatsAppChannel` never composes a message: it picks a PRE-APPROVED template name from settings (WHATSAPP_TEMPLATE_DEADLINE / WHATSAPP_TEMPLATE_HIGH_MATCH) and fills four numbered variables (title, buyer, when-or-score, link), each collapsed to one line and clipped to 200 characters. It sends only when (1) the event is `deadline_reminder` or `high_fit_match` — SPEC 7's only two WhatsApp rows, (2) the tenant's region is `in`, and (3) the user has `phone_e164` + `phone_verified_at`. Any gate that fails is a SKIPPED delivery (no retry, no email fallback), and no network call is made. There is no phone-VERIFICATION flow yet: `phone_verified_at` is set out of band until the settings UI owns it.
- OQ-125 (§7 / M6-05 receipt authentication) POST /api/v1/webhooks/whatsapp/{provider} is public like the billing webhooks. Gupshup signs the raw body with HMAC-SHA256, which `WHATSAPP_WEBHOOK_SECRET` verifies; Twilio's real scheme signs the request URL plus the sorted POST parameters, which needs the public URL the request arrived on, so for now a Twilio receipt is verified against the same shared secret in `X-Twilio-Signature`. With no secret configured, receipts are accepted unsigned — acceptable because a receipt can only ever move a `notification_deliveries` row we created, addressed by its `provider_ref`, and never backwards (a late `delivered` cannot demote an `opened`). The route is added to the audit middleware's asserted exempt set for the same reason the billing webhooks are (no user, no tenant).
- OQ-126 (M6-05 the one derived infra/ change) `app/core/config.SECRET_SETTINGS` gained gupshup_api_key, twilio_auth_token and whatsapp_webhook_secret, and `tests/unit/test_cloudrun_manifests.py` regenerates `infra/cloudrun/**` and fails on drift. The 14 generated manifests were therefore re-rendered with `python -m app.jobs.generate_cloudrun` and committed — the only infra/ change in this branch and a purely additive, derived one (three more `${SECRET_PREFIX}-*` secretKeyRefs per service/job), exactly as the M4 worktree did for its three new credentials.
- OQ-127 (§9 / M6-03 "in the user's time zone") A deadline is ONE instant, so the ladder is computed on absolute UTC moments and a US reader and an Indian reader of the same key date are reminded at the same second — each simply sees their own clock, rendered by `core.display_time` (the payload carries the buyer's `due_display`, and every Recipient carries their own tz). Reading "times computed in the user's tz" as per-user firing times would mean sending the same 24-hour reminder twice for one deadline. What IS per user is quiet hours: each recipient's delivery instant goes through `notify.scheduling.deliver_at`, whose 72-hour urgency override means the late rungs are never deferred.
- OQ-128 (§9 / M6-03 idempotency and the overdue cap) A rung's `sent_at` is stamped in the SAME transaction as its dispatch, so two overlapping five-minute ticks cannot send it twice (the Dispatcher's idempotency key, `<date>:<label>:<epoch>`, is a second belt). Rungs already in the past when a date is created are never generated, so a notice opened two days before its deadline gets no "7 days to go". Overdue rungs are added lazily, at most `MAX_OVERDUE_PER_PASS` (3) per tick, so an imported deadline from months ago produces a handful of reminders rather than hundreds. A rung that comes due once the pursuit has left the open stages records `skipped_reason` instead of sending.
- OQ-129 (§9 / M6-03 what escalation means) Level 0 reaches the pursuit owner, level 1 adds EVERY bid manager in the tenant, level 2 adds every tenant owner — the roles, not one named person, because the data model has no "the bid manager of this pursuit". Acknowledging the key date drops the rung back to level 0 at send time (the level is recomputed from the acknowledgement when the rung fires, not when it was created), so the owner still gets their reminder and nobody is escalated to.
- OQ-130 (§4.1 / M6-06 what an expired credential blocks) Only an expired SAM registration (US) or DSC (IN) sets `company_profiles.blocked_for_bids`; an expired GeM id, certification or insurance policy is a reminder, not a block, because the tender decides whether it matters (that is the eligibility engine's job, OQ-23). The block is recomputed from scratch on every daily run, so renewing a credential clears it the next day without a second endpoint. A blocked profile answers 409 {error: profile_blocked_for_bids, reason} on POST /opportunities/{id}/pursue but WATCHING is still allowed — watching starts no bid. There is no manual override route yet.
- OQ-131 (§7 / M6-06 one reminder per window) The 60/30/7 reminders are de-duplicated by the notification idempotency key (`expiry:<item id>:<window>`), not by a column on each credential table: a job that runs twice, or catches up after a missed day, sends nothing extra, and crossing into a tighter window is a different key so it sends again. `window_for` reports the TIGHTEST window that applies, so a job that skipped a week says "7 days", not "30". The stale-pursuit nudge is keyed `stale:<pursuit>:<date>` (once a day) and the matrix re-check by the opportunity version.
- OQ-132 (§9 / M6-06 what counts as "activity") `pursuits.activity_at` is bumped by `services.pursuits.touch` from every stage move, board action, key-date create/edit, task create/close and comment. Agent runs do NOT bump it yet — `AgentRunner` has no pursuit handle in its finish path; wire it there (or from the pipeline job) when the M5 branch lands, otherwise a pursuit worked on only by agents can look stale after five days.
- OQ-133 (§9 / M6-08 what "pipeline value" counts) `pipeline_value_by_stage` covers the OPEN stages only — a submitted, awarded, lost or cancelled pursuit is not pipeline. The figure per pursuit is the notice's `estimated_value_max_usd`, falling back to `estimated_value_min_usd`; a notice with neither contributes nothing rather than a guess. BOTH currencies are derived from that one USD figure (`core.dashboard.usd_to` divides by the same FX table `core.money.to_usd` multiplies by), so the USD and INR columns can never disagree — the INR column is therefore a converted total, not a sum of native INR values. A missing INR rate degrades to 0.00 rather than failing the home screen.
- OQ-134 (§9 / M6-08 hours saved and "packages submitted") Nothing measures hours saved, so `avg_hours_saved_per_package` is the `HOURS_SAVED_PER_PACKAGE` setting (default 20) and `hours_saved_total` is that times the packages submitted; the response carries `hours_saved_basis` saying so in words, so the number is never mistaken for a measurement. "Submitted" counts pursuits at `submitted`, `awarded` and `lost` — a package that went on to be won still went out of the door. Every rate (win rate, alert precision) is null, not 0, when its denominator is empty.
- OQ-135 (§9 / M6-08 alert precision before match_feedback exists) `match_feedback` belongs to the parallel matching branch and does not exist here. `services/dashboard.feedback_precision` checks `information_schema` first and answers (None, 0) when the table is absent, and catches a ProgrammingError when it is present with a different shape — so `alert_precision` is null today and lights up at merge without a code change, provided the table keeps a `verdict` column whose useful values are among ('useful', 'good', 'relevant'). Reconcile that column name with the matching branch at merge.
- OQ-105 (§12 / M7-10 what a scaled load run is allowed to prove) `scripts/load/score.py` enforces SPEC 12's 600 s gate only at (or within 10% of) 50k x 200; below that it scales the budget with the pair count but never below a 180 s floor, because at `--scale 0.1` a linear budget would be 6 s and a two-core GitHub runner would fail it for being a two-core GitHub runner. The nightly `load.yml` therefore runs the SCALED smoke (5,000 notices x 20 profiles) and is a regression detector, not the SPEC measurement; the production-shaped number is a developer-machine `make load-full` recorded in `scripts/load/README.md` and below. `budget_reason` in every score report says which of the two you are reading, so a green nightly can never be mistaken for a green SPEC target.
- OQ-106 (§12 / §10.3 `min_score` misses the search p95 target at full size) At 50k notices x 200 profiles (2.07 M `matches` rows) the 500-query search run measures p95 538 ms against SPEC 12's 500 ms. The split in the report's `by_kind` block is unambiguous: the 408 queries WITHOUT `min_score` are p50 22.5 ms / p95 258.5 ms, the 92 queries WITH it are p50 144.5 ms / p95 1,594 ms / p99 4,069 ms. `services.matching.read.with_min_score` is an `EXISTS` over `matches`, and the only usable index there is `ix_matches_opportunity_id`, so the planner probes it once per candidate notice (25,000 loops) and throws away ~43 rows per probe on `tenant_id` and `score` — 1.06 M buffer reads for one page of results. The fix is a composite index on `matches (tenant_id, opportunity_id, score)` (or `(tenant_id, score)` with the opportunity id included), which is a migration on an M4 table and therefore NOT made here; M7-10 owns the measurement, not the schema. Until it lands, `GET /opportunities?min_score=` is the one query shape that does not meet SPEC 12 at production size — everything else (FTS, NAICS, region, type, status, deep paging) is inside the budget on the same corpus.
- OQ-107 (§12 / M7-10 how the load script shards, and what it deliberately leaves out) OQ-95 said the batch had to be fanned out; the script does it with a `spawn` process pool over `MatchScorer.rescore_profile` (one profile per shard entry, one asyncio loop and one `Database` per worker) rather than through Celery, because a load measurement must not also be a measurement of the broker, the prefetch settings and the worker concurrency — those belong to a soak test. The consequence is that the 461 s number is the SCORER's number and a production run through `bidradar.score_batch` on Cloud Run workers will differ by whatever the queue costs. Stage 3 (the LLM rationale) is excluded for the same reason: `rationale=None`, so no network call is inside the 600 s. Notifications are excluded too (a bare `EventBus()` with no subscribers), which matters because a real 50k x 200 batch would otherwise create 29k high-band alerts.
- OQ-136 (§12 / M7-11 what "automated" means on the India checklist) The runbook's Automated? column names a test only where the test exercises the SAME code path the manual step would: date/money/Hindi/dedupe/eligibility rows are fully automated, but the WhatsApp, SES, Razorpay and residency rows are split in two — the CODE half is automated (gates, region choice, provider choice, GST arithmetic, the Terraform declaration) and the WORLD half (a template approved by Meta, a mail-tester score, a test card through the hosted page, `gcloud storage buckets describe`) stays manual with the status "manual-pending". A row is never marked automated-green because a neighbouring assertion passes; of the 30 rows (26 checklist bullets plus the 4 India-relevant MVP acceptance criteria) 14 are automated-green and 16 are manual-pending, and the runbook says which is which rather than implying staging-in has been exercised. Nothing in staging-in has ever been deployed, so every manual row starts pending.
- OQ-137 (§12 / M7-11 `make india-check` deferred) The task asked for a `make india-check` target, but the Makefile held another agent's uncommitted M7-10 load-target edits at commit time and CLAUDE.md's "never -A" rule makes staging a half-written shared file worse than skipping it. The runbook carries the exact three-line stanza to paste under `acceptance` (plus the `.PHONY` and `help` additions) and both the runbook and the test module's docstring give the raw `uv run pytest tests/integration/test_india_checklist.py` command, so the checklist is runnable today and the target is a one-minute follow-up. Same reasoning for the README testing section: it was mid-edit by the same agent and was left alone.
- OQ-138 (§5.3 / §12 / M7-11 "tender fee extracted" is only half true) `opportunities.tender_fee` exists, `core.money.parse_inr` reads every portal spelling of it, `services.ingest` copies it from `OpportunityIn`, and `core.compliance` turns a stored figure into a required payment item — but NO adapter and NOT the GeM extractor ever set it: `gem_extraction._apply` writes `emd_amount` and the value columns only, and cppp/gepnic/gem map no fee field. So the automated test proves the column and the checklist item work end to end from a value the caller supplies, and the checklist row stays manual-pending for a real tender. Closing it means either a fee field in the GeM extraction schema (it is on the bid PDF) or a CPPP/GePNIC detail-page selector; until then the compliance matrix simply does not list a tender fee for a real Indian notice.
- OQ-139 (§11 / §12 / M7-11 residency is asserted as declared, not as deployed) `test_residency_staging_in_declares_asia_south1_...` parses the HCL: staging-in's region is asia-south1, residency "in", and the bucket, its CMEK key, Cloud SQL, the backup location and Secret Manager replication all follow the single `var.region`, with the `check "residency_matches_region"` block refusing a mismatched plan. That is the strongest claim a test with no GCP project can make. The live locations (`terraform output residency`, `gcloud storage buckets describe`, `gcloud sql instances describe`, and the secret replica list) are a manual runbook row and are the ones DPDP actually cares about; the automated test exists so the live check cannot come as a surprise, not to replace it.
- OQ-140 (§9 / M6-09 what a "gate badge" can say today) SPEC 9 wants the board to show why a card is stuck, but the API exposes no gate field: `PursuitListItem` carries `stage`, `decision` and `matrix_recheck_required` and nothing else. `lib/pursuits/stages.gateBadges` therefore DERIVES the three badges the data supports — "Needs bid decision" for a card sitting in `bid_decision` with `decision` still null (Gate 1), "Needs package approval" for any card in `final_approval` (Gate 2 is the stage itself, so the badge is a label, not a fact about an approval request), and "Re-check compliance" from `matrix_recheck_required` (M6-06). When the drafting branch lands a real gate/approval-request record, gateBadges is the one function to rewrite; the board, the card and the unit tests all read it.
- OQ-141 (§10.4 screen 7 / M6-09 the calendar has no aggregate route) There is no tenant-wide key-dates endpoint: `GET /pursuits/{id}/dates` is per pursuit and the only cross-pursuit aggregate, `GET /calendar.ics`, is per USER (OQ-121) and is iCal, not JSON. `/app/calendar` therefore fans out — one paged `GET /pursuits`, then one dates call per card, six at a time — and caps at 200 pursuits with a visible notice. That is 1 + N requests for a month view, which is fine at MVP size and wrong at scale; the fix is a `GET /api/v1/key-dates?from=&to=` returning the tenant's dates in one page, and `loadCalendarEvents` is the single call site to swap. A pursuit whose dates cannot be read is skipped rather than blanking the month.
- OQ-142 (§9 / M6-09 how much board a board may draw) The table paginates (25/50/100 a page, `page`/`page_size` in the URL); the board cannot, because a Kanban column with a "next page" button is a lie about what is in the stage. The board therefore loads EVERY page of the current filter at the API's maximum `page_size` of 200 and stops at 500 cards with a notice naming the total and pointing at the filters and the table. The column counts stay honest either way: `by_stage` is computed by the server over the whole filtered set, not the page (M6-01), so a capped board still shows "Drafting 812".
- OQ-143 (§9 / M6-09 two filters the API does not have) SPEC 9's filter list is owner, due date, value, region and stage, and `GET /pursuits` takes all five (plus `watch`). Two gaps are closed on the client and marked as such: `owner=unassigned` has no server equivalent, so it is dropped from the request and applied to the returned rows (correct on the board, which holds every page; on the table it filters within the page, which is why the row count can fall short of `total` — a server-side `owner=none` would fix it). And `stage` is a comma-separated list on the wire but a single-select in the bar, because a multi-select stage filter on a board whose columns ARE the stages is a control that fights itself; the serializer already round-trips a list, so a multi-select is a UI change only.
- OQ-144 (§9 / M6-09 Home no longer guesses) `lib/dashboard/api` used to tolerate three shapes of `pipeline_value_by_stage` and turn a 404 into a "metrics arrive with the pursuits milestone" placeholder, because M6-08 was not on main. It is now, so the reader is typed against `DashboardOut` and a 404 is an error like any other. Two SPEC 9 numbers are rendered as words rather than zero when their denominator is empty (OQ-134): `win_rate` null shows "Not enough decisions yet" and `alert_precision` null shows "Collecting feedback". Hours saved always renders `hours_saved_basis` beside it, so an estimate is never read as a measurement.
- OQ-145 (M6 OQ-117 / §10.2 tasks and comments, decided at the m5b merge) SPEC 10.2's `tasks` and `comments` (migration 0007, app/models/collab.py) are the tables; M6-07's `pursuit_tasks` / `pursuit_comments` are gone from 0011 and their columns (detail, created_by, completed_at/by, updated_at, resolved_by, the status/source/target_type CHECKs and the assignee and target indexes) moved onto 0007's two tables, so every column lives in exactly one migration and the drift test stays exact. The route table keeps M6-07's richer CRUD (GET/POST /pursuits/{id}/tasks with status and assignee filters, PATCH/DELETE .../{task_id}; GET/POST /pursuits/{id}/comments with target_type / target_id / unresolved filters, PATCH/DELETE .../{comment_id}) plus M5-16's POST .../comments/{comment_id}/resolve, all in api/v1/collab.py; the M5-16 comment routes in api/v1/drafts.py are deleted and `tests/unit/test_route_table.py` walks app.routes and fails on any duplicate (method, path). The comment vocabulary is M6's seven anchors, so M5's `draft` target is `draft_section` and its `scorecard` target is `artifact`; M5-16's rule that a comment must resolve to a row of THIS pursuit now guards all of them, and `CommentListOut` carries `unresolved_count` and M5-16's `open_count` so neither reader broke. The drafters call `services.collab.create_task_from_placeholder`, keyed on (pursuit, `draft:<section>`, question) so a re-run re-uses the task instead of asking twice (OQ-119), and it takes `scope=` like every other allowlisted write (OQ-112).
- OQ-146 (§8 / §9 the two gates move the stage through one rule set, decided at the m5b merge) POST /pursuits/{id}/decision, POST /pursuits/{id}/approve-package and POST /pursuits/{id}/mark-final all go through `services.pursuits.move_stage` and therefore `core.pursuit_stages.can_transition`, instead of M5-06's own two-rule `check_stage_transition` (deleted). A gate is not a drag, so `advance_to` walks the ladder one rung at a time — recording "bid" on a pursuit still at Identified moves it through Qualifying and Bid decision into Drafting rather than answering "cannot skip qualifying" — and the whole path is checked before any of it is applied, so a refusal names the move that was asked for and leaves the row untouched. Gate 2 therefore presupposes Gate 1: approving a package on a pursuit nobody decided to bid on is refused by the Drafting gate, which is why the isolation fixture now seeds a decided pursuit. All three also call `services.pursuits.touch`, and so does every agent run at its start and its finish (OQ-132 closed).

<!-- from PROGRESS.m5b.md (M5 part 2 worktree); its OQ numbers are that worktree's own series and overlap the M6/M7 numbers above -->
- OQ-100 (§8 / §9 Gate 1 mechanics) The pipeline registry now carries `GATES = {bid_no_bid: gate1}` and `plan_steps(step, gates_cleared=...)` ends the plan after a gated step, for a single-step request as well as for `all`. A gate is cleared by the pursuit's own state rather than by a run parameter: `services/pursuits.cleared_gates(pursuit)` returns `("gate1",)` once `pursuits.decision == 'bid'`, and `jobs/run_agents` reads the pursuit before planning, so re-dispatching the SAME run id after the decision simply plans past the gate and skips the steps already done. A `no_bid` clears nothing and resumes nothing. M5-10 adds `GATE_2` after `red_team` the same way.
- OQ-101 (§8 agent 4 scorecard vs `bid_no_bid_weights`) SPEC's scorecard names the fifth criterion "value" while the design calls the field `value_fit`; the Scorecard schema keeps `value_fit` (clearer next to `win_probability`) and `Scorecard.scores()` maps it onto the preferences key `value`, so the profile's weights validate unchanged. The model's own `recommendation` is stored verbatim next to the weighted total and `suggested_recommendation` (bid >= 65, watch >= 45, else no_bid); a disagreement is shown rather than resolved, because the weights are the tenant's policy and the reasons are the model's. A profile whose stored weights no longer validate falls back to the defaults with a warning instead of failing the run.
- OQ-102 (§10.3 PATCH /pursuits/{id} before M6-01) The acceptance line "stage cannot move to Drafting without a bid decision" needs a stage route, so this pass adds a deliberately small `PATCH /api/v1/pursuits/{id}` (stage, owner_user_id; bid manager / owner) whose only rule is `services/pursuits.check_stage_transition`: an unknown stage is 422, `drafting` without `decision == 'bid'` is 409. M6-01 extends that one helper with the full board rules (ordering, key dates, drag-and-drop) instead of adding a second rule set in the route.
- OQ-105 (§8 agent 8 "drafts auto-revised once") "Once" is enforced per SECTION, not per run: the revision writes `flags.red_team.revised = true` on the new version, and a later red-team run sees `already_revised` and turns that section's findings into comments instead of rewriting it again. A human edit (author user) clears the marker, so a section a person reworked is eligible for one more agent revision on the next review. The run always writes a NEW `red_team` artifact version, so the history shows both reviews.
- OQ-106 (§8 which findings really got fixed) The report cannot be re-run cheaply, so `core.red_team.remaining_issues` decides deterministically where it can and trusts the revision only where it must: a `page_limit` is resolved when the re-measured body fits, an `unsupported_claim` when its verbatim sentence is gone from the new body (or, with no sentence, when the grounding validator reports 0 unsupported claims), and `non_compliant` / `missing_requirement` / `clarity` only when the revision listed the finding in `addressed`. Everything else becomes a review comment, so a revision that claims a fix it did not make still leaves the finding visible.
- OQ-107 (§8 "page-limit overruns") Page counts are computed, never modelled: `core.page_estimate` divides the word count by the words one page holds under the solicitation's own font size and margins (500 words at 12 pt with 1 inch margins, scaled by 1/size^2 and by the text area, clamped to 120-1500). The prompt explicitly tells the reviewer NOT to judge length and every `page_limit` issue the model returns is dropped and replaced. A whole-package overrun against `format_rules.page_limit` is attached to the LONGEST section, because that is the one a reviewer cuts first.
- OQ-108 (§8 Gate 2 vs "mark final") Gate 2 is `POST /pursuits/{id}/approve-package` (bid manager / tenant owner): it records `pursuits.package_approved_by/at`, marks every section approved, moves the stage to `final_approval`, audits `pursuit.package_approved` and resumes the paused run. It deliberately does NOT make the export final -- SPEC 11's "internal footer until a human marks it final" is a second, separate step (`package_final`, M5-13), so approving the content and releasing the document are two decisions.
- OQ-109 (§8 "human-in-the-loop edits are diffed") The diff is taken over `body_text`, not `body_html`, and `core.diffs.split_for_diff` collapses runs of whitespace and splits paragraphs over 200 characters into sentences before diffing: a TipTap editor re-wraps markup on every keystroke, so an HTML diff would record reflow as feedback and a paragraph-level diff would only ever say "the paragraph changed". A save whose rendered text is unchanged writes no `draft_feedback` row at all.
- OQ-110 (§8 what "feedback" a future prompt may see) `services/draft_feedback.recent_examples(session, tenant_id, section_kind, k)` keys on the outline SECTION ID (technical-approach, past-performance ...), which is stable across pursuits, and returns only rows whose `from_author` is `agent` -- a writer editing another writer's wording is not a lesson about how the agent should have drafted it. It filters on `tenant_id` on top of the session's RLS, so the query is wrong twice before it could ever read another tenant's proposal text; an integration test asks for tenant B's id from tenant A's session and gets nothing.
- OQ-111 (§11 "tool access for agents is limited") No agent runs a tool LOOP today: every step is one structured-output call, so the "tool set" is enforced two ways instead. `app/agents/tools.py` defines `PursuitTools` (kb_search / read_document / read_requirements, the only reads a loop would ever be given, each bound to the run's pursuit) and the ALLOWLISTS that `tests/unit/test_agent_tool_scope.py` enforces by parsing every module under app/agents: the models an agent may `session.add` / `delete` (OpportunityDocument, Requirement, ComplianceItem — the runner additionally owns AgentRun / AgentStep / UsageLedger), the service functions it may call to write (save_version, create_task, add_comment, store_artifact, parse_and_store) and EVERY name it may import from app.services at all. The scanner has its own self-tests over synthetic source, so it cannot pass vacuously.
- OQ-112 (§11 cross-PURSUIT writes inside one tenant) RLS stops a cross-tenant write; nothing stopped an agent writing to a sibling pursuit of the same tenant. `PursuitScope(tenant_id, pursuit_id, run_id)` now travels on every `StepContext` (the runner builds it from the agent_runs row) and each write helper takes `scope=` and calls `enforce`, raising `ScopeViolationError` (aliased `ScopeViolation`; ruff N818 wants the suffix). The scope is optional so API callers, already authorised by the route's role check, pass none.
- OQ-113 (§11/§12 what the injection eval can assert without a model) The suite checks the two defences separately. FRAMING is pure and covers EVERY implemented agent by calling its real prompt builder with stub inputs: the preamble is in the system prompt and the attacker's marker appears only inside an `<untrusted>` span of system+user+cache blocks. OUTPUT is checked where an agent runs without a database (extractor, gem_extract) by scripting a model that OBEYS the injection — it returns the baseline items plus a poisoned one — and asserting the validators drop the poison so the stored output is identical to the clean baseline. `assert_only_inside_untrusted` has negative self-tests, so a regression that stops wrapping text fails the suite rather than passing quietly.
- OQ-114 (§8 "DOCX in the tenant's template" default) The bundled default is built in memory by `services/exports.default_template_bytes()` (a python-docx `Document()` with Calibri 11) rather than committed as a binary .docx: a binary asset in the repo cannot be reviewed in a diff, and python-docx's own default package is exactly what a plain template is. A tenant's uploaded template (`profile_files` kind `template`) is used when there is one, and an upload python-docx cannot open falls back to the default with a warning rather than failing the export.
- OQ-115 (§8 PDF rendering) The PDF is converted from the DOCX with LibreOffice (`soffice --headless --convert-to pdf`, fixed argv, no shell, an isolated HOME) when `soffice` is on PATH, and otherwise laid out directly with PyMuPDF, which is already a dependency (reportlab would have added one for the same job). The path that actually ran is stored in `exports.renderer`, so a deployment without LibreOffice shows up in the row instead of silently producing a different-looking file; a test pins the fallback by patching `soffice_path` to None.
- OQ-116 (§11 "internal footer until a human marks it final" vs Gate 2) Two separate switches, deliberately: `POST /pursuits/{id}/approve-package` (Gate 2, M5-10) approves the CONTENT, and `POST /pursuits/{id}/mark-final` (409 before Gate 2) releases the DOCUMENT by setting `pursuits.package_final`. An export already rendered keeps the footer it was rendered with -- `exports.final` records it -- because the file a reviewer downloaded must not change meaning after the fact.
- OQ-117 (§8 ZIP naming from an untrusted rule) `format_rules.file_naming` was copied out of a solicitation PDF by a model, so `core/exports.ExportNaming` treats it as untrusted: known placeholders are substituted, unknown ones are dropped rather than guessed, and the result always goes through `sanitize_filename`, which strips path separators and forces the real extension. A rule of `../../../etc/{company}` produces a plain file name inside the archive.
- OQ-118 (§12 golden notices) All 19 new notices are SYNTHETIC: written for this eval in the shape of the real thing (a DoD RFP with Sections L and M, a GeM bid with EMD and turnover in a Hindi/English mix, a GePNIC two-cover tender) rather than copied from a published notice, because redistributing scraped portal text is exactly what SPEC 11's source-compliance section says to get an opinion on first. `evals/golden/make_golden.py` builds each PDF, its labels and its recorded answer from one definition in `us_items.py` / `in_items.py`, so the three can never drift apart.
- OQ-119 (§12 what a recorded answer may be) A replay of the labels would make the bars a tautology, so the generator derives each recorded answer from the labels and then damages it on purpose: every requirement is restated (`restate()` keeps the subject noun -- dropping it cost short sentences enough similarity that the scorer measured the paraphraser rather than the extractor), one label per item is missed, a real but non-binding sentence is over-extracted, one obligation is repeated for the deduper and two items are emitted that the citation validator must reject (a page outside the batch, an invented quote). `test_golden.py` asserts recall and precision stay strictly below 100% and that the recorded texts are not a subset of the labels.
- OQ-120 (§12 "eligibility extraction exact match") The bar names turnover, EMD and experience, which are Indian fields, so the metric is region-aware: an Indian item must yield all three numbers EXACTLY (parsed out of the extracted eligibility requirements with `core.money.parse_inr`), while a US item must state its NAICS, its set-aside and the SAM obligation. A notice with neither -- the city RFP -- declares `eligibility_keywords` instead, so every item is measured on something. The checker has negative unit tests, because a metric that only ever reports 100% is indistinguishable from a broken one.
